# Academic coverage map

Generated by `scripts/build_reference_docs.py` from the reference catalogue, the topic coverage records and the
generated knowledge graph; edit the JSON sources, not this file.

This map connects **MIT course → MIT topic → AIPE knowledge module → AIPE lesson / lab**. It separates two
different facts: *academic coverage* (a verified MIT course teaches the topic) and *content completion* (an
AIPE lesson with that status exists). A topic covered by MIT and marked `planned` here has no AIPE lesson yet.

Coverage labels: `core` the course's central subject matter; `partial` substantial but not central; `introductory`
brief treatment; `background` assumed or tangential. Lesson statuses: `available`, `draft`, `outline`, or `planned`
(no lesson). MIT OpenCourseWare is an external resource; AIPE Academy is not affiliated with or endorsed by MIT.

## 2. MIT course → MIT topic → AIPE module

Topic titles are short public listing titles (at most twelve words), grouped by the official page they were read from; nothing else from the courses is reproduced. Mapping rationales, background-level mappings and per-page resource summaries are in `references/mit-ocw-coverage.json`.

### MIT 18.01SC — Single Variable Calculus (Fall 2010)

MIT 18.01SC (OCW Scholar edition, Fall 2010, Prof. David Jerison) is a self-contained single-variable calculus course for independent learners. It runs through five units: differentiation (definition, rules, implicit differentiation, exponentials and inverses), applications of differentiation (approximation, curve sketching, optimization, related rates, Newton's method, Mean Value Theorem, antiderivatives and a first look at separable differential equations), the definite integral and its applications (Riemann sums, both Fundamental Theorems, areas, volumes, averages, probability, numerical integration), techniques of integration (trigonometric methods, partial fractions, integration by parts, arc length, parametric and polar curves), and infinite processes (L'Hospital's rule, improper integrals, series and Taylor series). The material is organised as 102 sequential sessions (Sessions 1…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): High-school algebra and trigonometry (the syllabus names these as the only prerequisites).; Earlier exposure to calculus is described as helpful but not necessary.

### MIT 18.02SC — Multivariable Calculus (Fall 2010)

18.02SC is the OCW Scholar (self-study) edition of MIT's second-semester freshman calculus, taught by Prof. Denis Auroux: vector and matrix algebra, calculus of functions of several variables (partial derivatives, gradient, chain rule, optimisation with and without constraints), double and triple integrals in several coordinate systems, and vector calculus in two and three dimensions culminating in Green's, Stokes' and the divergence theorems, with a short physics-applications coda on Maxwell's equations. It is organised into 4 units, 12 parts and 102 numbered sessions, of which Sessions 22-23, 45-46 and 73 are unit-exam reviews and Sessions 99-102 are final-exam reviews. A typical session (Session 1 sampled) links a segmented lecture-video clip (recorded fall 2007) with board images, lecture notes, worked examples, a recitation video and problems with solutions; the syllabus says most…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): 18.01 Single Variable Calculus (MIT's first-semester calculus) is the only stated prerequisite

### MIT 18.03SC — Differential Equations (Fall 2011)

18.03SC is the OCW Scholar self-study edition of MIT's undergraduate differential-equations course. It is built from four units: first-order ODEs (analytic, geometric and numerical methods, complex arithmetic, sinusoidal and exponential inputs, autonomous equations), second-order constant-coefficient linear equations (characteristic equation, damped oscillators, exponential response, gain and phase lag, resonance, frequency response, an LRC-circuit application), Fourier series and Laplace transform (periodic input, step and delta functions, impulse response, convolution, transfer functions, poles), and first-order 2x2 systems (matrix methods, eigenvalues, phase portraits, matrix exponentials, linearisation of nonlinear systems, limit cycles and chaos). The navigation lists 39 session pages plus three unit exams and a final. Most session pages link course-note PDFs, clips from Prof. Matt…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): Single-variable calculus (MIT 18.01) is a prerequisite; OCW users are pointed to 18.01SC as preparation.; Multivariable calculus (MIT 18.02) is a corequisite, i.e. it may be taken at the same time; from it the course assumes familiarity with vectors, basic matrix a…; The syllabus names no textbook; the course is meant to be worked through on one's own from its notes, videos, problem sets and exams.

### MIT 18.06SC — Linear Algebra (Fall 2011)

18.06SC is the OCW Scholar self-study version of MIT's undergraduate linear algebra course (Prof. Gilbert Strang, Fall 2011), organised in three units: (I) solving Ax = b, elimination, LU, inverses and the four fundamental subspaces; (II) orthogonality, projections and least squares, determinants, eigenvalues, diagonalization and the matrix exponential exp(At) for systems of differential equations, Markov matrices and Fourier series; (III) symmetric and positive definite matrices, complex matrices and the FFT, similar matrices and Jordan form, SVD, linear transformations, change of basis and pseudoinverses. Each lecture session pairs a lecture video with a lecture-summary PDF, suggested textbook sections and a recitation (problem-solving) video; 31 of the 36 lecture sessions also carry a problem set with solutions (the overview session, the three exam-review sessions and the final revie…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): 18.02 Multivariable Calculus is the formal prerequisite for MIT students taking 18.06, but the syllabus says calculus itself is not needed to follow this cours…; Learners need working familiarity with vectors, matrices and 3-D coordinates; the syllabus notes this is taught early in 18.02 and is covered again within this…; The Instructor Insights page lists the prerequisite as the Mathematics GIR (MIT general institute requirement).

### MIT 8.02 — Physics II: Electricity and Magnetism (Spring 2007)

MIT 8.02 (Spring 2007) is MIT's first-year electricity-and-magnetism course, the second half of the introductory physics sequence. It is run in the TEAL studio format: short lecture segments alternate with tabletop experiments, electronically polled concept questions and small-group problem work, and one one-hour session a week is given over to problem solving. Its 36 sessions (26 marked Class and 10 marked Problem solving in the Class Topics table) run from electrostatics (charge, field, potential, Gauss's law, conductors and capacitors) through current and DC circuits, magnetostatics (Biot-Savart, Ampere's law, forces and torques on charges, dipoles and current loops), Faraday's law, inductance and transformers, RC/RL/RLC transients and driven AC resonance, to Maxwell's equations, electromagnetic radiation and a closing session on interference. The OCW site offers lecture notes (14 co…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): 8.01 Physics I: Classical Mechanics is the only prerequisite the syllabus lists.

### MIT 6.002 — Circuits and Electronics (Spring 2007)

MIT 6.002 (Spring 2007, Prof. Anant Agarwal; the home page states the course materials were created collaboratively with Prof. Jeffrey H. Lang) is MIT's first undergraduate circuits course, built on the lumped circuit abstraction. Over 25 lectures and 26 recitations it moves from resistive network analysis (KVL/KCL, node method, superposition, Thevenin/Norton) through the digital abstraction and MOSFET switch and amplifier models (large- and small-signal), into capacitors, inductors and first- and second-order transients, then sinusoidal steady state, impedance methods and filters, operational-amplifier feedback circuits, and energy and power in digital/CMOS circuits, ending with a lecture on the limits of the lumped abstraction. Five lab assignments (Lab 0 familiarization; Thevenin/Norton equivalents and logic gates; MOSFET amplifiers and first-order circuits; second-order networks; a…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): MIT 8.02 Physics II (electricity and magnetism) is listed as a prerequisite on the syllabus page.; MIT 18.03 Differential Equations is listed as a prerequisite on the syllabus page.

### MIT 6.003 — Signals and Systems (Fall 2011)

MIT 6.003 Signals and Systems (Fall 2011, undergraduate, Prof. Dennis Freeman) is a one-semester course in signal and LTI-system analysis that develops the discrete-time and continuous-time cases side by side. The syllabus groups the material into two strands: ways of representing signals (singularity functions, complex exponentials, the Fourier, Laplace and Z transforms, and sampling) and ways of representing linear time-invariant systems (block diagrams, difference or differential equations, system functions with their poles and zeros, convolution sums and integrals, and the impulse, step and frequency responses). The 25 lecture titles run from DT and CT systems through the Z and Laplace transforms, convolution, frequency response, Bode plots and feedback/control to the four Fourier representations, sampling and quantization, modulation and an Audio CD capstone; MIT names control, com…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): The syllabus lists a single prerequisite: MIT subject 6.02 (Introduction to EECS II); No other prerequisite, corequisite or required mathematics background is listed on the syllabus or the course home page (the textbook, meeting format and gradi…

### MIT 6.013 — Electromagnetics and Applications (Fall 2005)

MIT 6.013 (Fall 2005, undergraduate; listed under Electrical Engineering and Computer Science and the Engineering Systems Division; instructors listed as Prof. David Staelin, Prof. Erich Ippen and Markus Zahn) is an applied electromagnetics course that builds Maxwell's equations from the Coulomb-Lorentz force law, through boundary conditions, potentials, media and the electro-/magneto-quasistatic approximations, and then applies them in six units: Maxwell's equations, plane waves, transmission lines and waveguides, fields and forces (field energy, virtual-work forces, rotating and self-excited machines), antennas and radiation, and acoustics. The course page names applications spanning wireless and optical communications, circuits and computer interconnects, microwave links and radar, sensors, MEMS, motors, and power generation and transmission. Delivery was 23 lectures and 25 recitatio…

### MIT 6.622 — Power Electronics (Spring 2023)

MIT 6.622 Power Electronics, Spring 2023, is a graduate subject taught by Prof. David Perreault on the modelling, analysis, design and control of switching circuits that convert electrical energy. Its 38 numbered lectures open with rectifiers, load regulation and power factor, continue with a four-lecture DC/DC block (inferred to be the non-isolated converter family, since isolated converters follow the magnetics block), four magnetics lectures, two lectures on isolated DC/DC converters, switching losses and snubbers, and thermal modelling and heat sinking; the second half covers a three-lecture inverter block, switched-mode rectifiers, three-phase systems and three-phase inverters, converter control including current-mode control, EMI filters (including common-mode and differential-mode noise), switched-capacitor converters, soft switching, resonant power conversion with matching netwo…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): 6.2500 Nanoelectronics and Computing Systems (the only prerequisite named on the syllabus); Inference (not stated on any page): 6.2500 is an MIT EECS devices-and-circuits subject, and the lecture sequence presupposes fluency with circuit analysis, sem…

### MIT 6.334 — Power Electronics (Spring 2007)

MIT 6.334 Power Electronics (Spring 2007, graduate, Prof. David Perreault) is a one-semester graduate course on using electronic circuits for energy conversion and control, worth 6 engineering design points. Its 37 calendar lectures run from analysis methods and rectifier basics (AC-side reactance, power factor and distortion) through DC-DC converters, magnetics, isolated DC/DC converters, DC-AC inverters and PWM, EMI filtering, loss computation and snubbers, soft switching, thermal design and resonant/RF converters, to averaged modelling and control, three-phase (multi-pulse) rectifier systems, practical layout and gate-drive issues, switched-mode rectifiers and a closing design example. Assessment is weekly problem sets (40%), two evening quizzes (50%, no final exam) and a design project (10%); the required text is Kassakian, Schlecht and Verghese, Principles of Power Electronics, and…

### MIT 6.012 — Microelectronic Devices and Circuits (Fall 2005)

MIT presents 6.012 Microelectronic Devices and Circuits (Fall 2005, undergraduate, Prof. Jesús del Alamo) as the entry course for its EECS devices/circuits/systems concentration. It starts from semiconductor carrier physics (electron and hole populations, doping with donors and acceptors, drift and diffusion), builds the electrostatics of p-n junctions and MOS structures, derives MOSFET current-voltage characteristics and equivalent-circuit (large-signal and small-signal) models, then applies them to NMOS/CMOS inverter logic (transfer characteristics, delay, scaling, dynamic power). A second device block covers p-n diode I-V, charge storage and diffusion capacitance, and the bipolar junction transistor; the final block treats MOSFET and BJT single-stage and multistage amplifiers, current sources and sinks, and amplifier frequency response (Miller effect, open-circuit time constants). As…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): MIT 6.003 listed as a corequisite (may be taken at the same time); the syllabus gives only the number, which is MIT's signals and systems subject (inference); MIT 8.02 (second-semester physics; the number MIT uses for electricity and magnetism, inference from the course number); MIT 18.03 (the number MIT uses for differential equations, inference from the course number)

### MIT 6.302 — Feedback Systems (Spring 2007)

MIT 6.302 Feedback Systems (Spring 2007, listed by OCW as Graduate; instructors James Roberge, Joel Dawson, Kent Lundberg) is an introductory feedback-control design course that this record characterises as classical and continuous-time (the record's own terms; no OCW page uses either). Judging from the session titles, it moves from the properties of feedback (desensitivity, linearisation), plant modelling and first/second-order dynamics, through stability tools (root locus, Nyquist criterion, Hall/Nichols charts, Bode gain and phase margins) to compensation design (lead/lag series compensation, minor-loop compensation, internal and external op-amp compensation, lightly damped plants) and finally nonlinear topics (describing functions, oscillators, conditional stability). Physical applications run through a servomotor laboratory (Lab 1A-1D) and a design project chosen from three options…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): Linear systems: officially MIT 6.003, 2.003 or 16.04 (the syllabus names the prerequisite areas as linear systems, circuits, physics and math).; Circuits with previous laboratory experience, e.g. 6.002, 6.071 or 16.04.; Introductory physics (8.01 and 8.02) should still be fresh.; Mathematics including some exposure to complex variables (18.04 is described as helpful but more than necessary).; Course reference text: Kent Lundberg, 'Notes on Feedback Systems' (title only; the syllabus states it is not available on OCW).; Numerical tools used in some problem sets: MATLAB and Octave (Octave is itself open-source software); xcircuit recommended for schematic drawing.

### MIT 6.061 — Introduction to Electric Power Systems (Spring 2011)

MIT 6.061 (with a graduate variant 6.690) is an advanced-undergraduate (OCW level: Undergraduate) introduction to electric power systems and electromechanical energy conversion, taught by Prof. James L. Kirtley Jr. in Spring 2011 with two 1.5-hour lectures per week. The 23-lecture calendar starts with a review of network theory, first-order transients (using buck and boost converters as examples) and sinusoidal steady state, then covers transmission lines, polyphase circuits, transformers and per-unit, symmetrical components, faults, protection, phase control and HVDC, and load flow. The second half turns to electromechanics (forces, virtual work) and synchronous, induction, DC and permanent-magnet machines including drive control, and closes with switching power-electronic circuits and inverters. Published materials are a 12-chapter set of course notes labelled '2007 Course Notes' (11…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): Circuits and electronics at the level of MIT 6.002; Electromagnetics at the level of MIT 6.013 (Electromagnetics and Applications); Instructor permission can substitute for the two subjects; anyone unsure whether their preparation suffices is directed to the instructor; In his introduction on the readings page the instructor pitches the course notes at readers who already know some circuit theory and electromagnetics, without…

### MIT 6.691 — Seminar in Electric Power Systems (Spring 2006)

MIT 6.691 (Spring 2006, graduate, Prof. James L. Kirtley Jr.) is a seminar on how bulk electric power systems are planned and operated, taught mainly from an engineering viewpoint with some attention to the economics of a restructured industry. Its self-described 'straw man' calendar runs from a review of complex power and polyphase systems through transmission-line two-port models and sending/receiving-end circle diagrams, transformers, Newton-Raphson and decoupled load flow, synchronous-machine modelling and per-unit models, electromechanical dynamics, voltage regulators and power system stabilizers, small-signal behaviour, generation costs and economic/optimal dispatch, symmetrical components, fault modelling and relaying. The home-page course description (the only fetched page that carries the 'core topics' list) additionally names, as core themes, system protection, multimachine dy…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): Prior exposure to power systems at the level of MIT 6.061, or alternatively strong network-theory preparation plus some controls and electromagnetics. The syll…; Economics background is called helpful because part of the seminar examines why the electricity industry was deregulated and restructured.; Rather than providing per-lecture notes, the Lecture Notes page refers readers to the 6.061 notes (same link target) for power-systems background.; Reference text named on the syllabus and assignments pages (bibliographic data only): Bergen and Vittal, Power Systems Analysis, 2nd ed., Prentice Hall, 1999;…

### MIT 6.685 — Electric Machines (Fall 2013)

A graduate course on electromechanics that uses electric machinery as its running example. It starts from field- and energy-based force calculation and magnetic circuits, then covers windings and air-gap fields and the major machine classes: round-rotor and two-reaction (Park transform) synchronous machines with their dynamics and simulation, induction machines (transformer model, squirrel-cage, drives and control, doubly-fed, single-phase, linear), DC commutator machines, variable-reluctance machines and permanent-magnet brushless DC motors, plus transformers, per-unit normalisation, machine sizing and power density, and design evaluation. The stated approach is deliberately classical and analytical, using numerical methods only when necessary; students are expected to be able to design the main classes of rotating and linear machines and to estimate their dynamic parameters. Research-…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): MIT's introductory electric power systems subject (6.061/6.690), or the instructor's consent; both the syllabus and the instructor-insights page give this as t…; The instructor-insights page describes the typical student background as some electromechanics (6.061) and circuits (6.002 Circuits and Electronics and 6.003 S…

### MIT 2.627 — Fundamentals of Photovoltaics (Fall 2013)

MIT 2.627 Fundamentals of Photovoltaics (Fall 2013; the OCW data.json also records the companion number 2.626, which no fetched HTML page shows) is an elective in MIT's undergraduate Energy Studies Minor covering the physics of photovoltaic conversion, how cells are made and evaluated, and the market and policy context in which PV technologies compete. The Fall 2013 calendar has 26 sessions in three units: Unit 1 device fundamentals (solar resource, light management, charge excitation, separation, transport and collection, a 1D device model, materials properties), Unit 2 commercial and emerging technologies (wafer silicon, thin films, efficiency limits, emerging concepts, a Fraunhofer CSE field trip), and Unit 3 cross-cutting themes (efficiency-loss troubleshooting; cost and manufacturability with an industry guest lecture on silicon cell manufacturing costs; modules, systems and reliab…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): The syllabus has no prerequisites section, and the word prerequisite does not occur on the syllabus or the home page. In a student-professor contract note the…; An ungraded Background Assessment Quiz is assigned at Lecture 1 (Calendar & Readings page; the PDF is listed on the Readings page) to gauge prior knowledge rat…; The syllabus names a free online PV textbook (Honsberg and Bowden, Photovoltaics: Devices, Systems and Applications CD-ROM) as the main text and lists 13 refer…

### MIT EC.S07 — Photovoltaic Solar Energy Systems (Fall 2004)

EC.S07 (Edgerton Center, Fall 2004, undergraduate, Prof. Louis Bucciarelli) is a one-session-per-week, two-hour class (the syllabus labels the meetings 'Lectures') on the behaviour and first-order conceptual design of photovoltaic systems, with the emphasis on stand-alone (off-grid) PV-plus-battery systems for any location in the US. The 14-session calendar moves from the solar resource (sun geometry, clearness index, US insolation databases and TMY data) through module I-V behaviour and array networking to stand-alone system elements, three sessions of stand-alone sizing (first-order estimation of average daily performance, then loss-of-load probability with a Markov process model and the effect of day-to-day correlation), evaluation of PV station data, two sessions on grid-connected systems (power conditioning, utility constraints, architectural/structural considerations) and cost/ben…

### MIT 22.081J — Introduction to Sustainable Energy (Fall 2010)

22.081J Introduction to Sustainable Energy (Fall 2010, undergraduate and graduate, listed under six departments: Nuclear Science and Engineering, Mechanical Engineering, Chemical Engineering, Civil and Environmental Engineering, Urban Studies and Planning, Engineering Systems Division) is a survey course that assesses present and future energy systems from resources through extraction, conversion and end use, judged against engineering, economic, political, social and environmental goals. Part I (sessions 1a-9b) builds a quantitative 'toolbox' (energy units and flows, conversion and efficiency, resource depletion, storage and transmission, systems analysis, electric power sector, electrical system dynamics, economic feasibility, thermodynamics, carbon policy); Part II (10-19b) surveys specific technologies (nuclear, fossil, wind, policy, transport, fusion, carbon management, geothermal,…

### MIT 8.21 — The Physics of Energy (Fall 2009)

MIT 8.21 The Physics of Energy (Physics Department, Fall 2009, undergraduate; instructors listed as Prof. Robert Jaffe and Prof. Washington Taylor) is a quantitative physics course on energy for students who already have introductory physics, calculus and chemistry: it asks where energy comes from, how it is extracted, moved, stored, lost and finally used, and which physical laws limit each step. Part I (Energy and its Uses) covers energy units and scales, mechanical, thermal and electromagnetic energy, an introduction to quantum energy levels, chemical energy and CO2 flows, entropy and temperature, heat engines, phase-change conversion with refrigeration and heat pumps, internal combustion engines, and steam and gas power cycles. Part II (Sources of Energy) covers fundamental forces, quantum mechanics for nuclear physics, nuclear forces and binding energy, fusion, fission and fission-r…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): Introductory physics: MIT 8.01 (classical mechanics) and 8.02 (electricity and magnetism); Mathematics: MIT 18.01 single-variable calculus and 18.02 multivariable calculus; Chemistry: Principles of Chemical Science (listed as 5.111 and 5.112) or Introduction to Solid State Chemistry (3.091); The syllabus states that students need not have taken advanced subjects in thermodynamics, quantum mechanics or nuclear physics beforehand; Target audience stated as MIT sophomores, juniors and seniors from any major; the course is not aimed specifically at physics majors

### MIT 10.626 — Electrochemical Energy Systems (Spring 2014)

MIT 10.626 Electrochemical Energy Systems, Spring 2014, Prof. Martin Bazant, Department of Chemical Engineering, graduate level. The Instructor Insights page states that the class contains both graduate and undergraduate students and that students taking the graduate version complete additional assignments; the course number 10.426 appears only in the course's data.json as recorded in references/mit-ocw-verification.json (observed.extra_course_numbers), not on any of the eight fetched HTML pages. The course teaches the physics and mathematical modelling of electrochemical energy conversion and storage in five parts: I Introduction (galvanic cells, conversion, storage), II Circuit Models (equivalent-circuit dynamics and impedance), III Thermodynamics (statistical thermodynamics and solution models, Nernst equation and open-circuit voltage, Pourbaix diagrams, metal-acid and Li-ion cells,…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): Syllabus page: 10.50 Analysis of Transport Phenomena (MIT's graduate transport course) is the only prerequisite listed.; Instructor Insights page: graduate students need 10.50 Analysis of Transport Phenomena or the instructor's permission; undergraduate students need 10.302 Trans…; Implied by the reading list and session titles (inference, not stated on any page): working knowledge of thermodynamics, physical chemistry, ordinary and parti…

### MIT 2.60J — Fundamentals of Advanced Energy Conversion (Spring 2020)

MIT 2.60J Fundamentals of Advanced Energy Conversion (Spring 2020; the OCW pages display only the number 2.60J, while the catalogue's verified OCW metadata lists the cross-listings 2.62J, 10.390J, 10.392J and 22.40J; instructor Prof. Ahmed F. Ghoniem; listed level Undergraduate, with the syllabus adding that a graduate version runs on identical content with a heavier assignment load) uses thermodynamics, chemical and electrochemical thermodynamics and transport to examine how energy is converted and stored, judging each process mainly by its efficiency, its delivered performance and its environmental footprint. ### MIT 15.031J — Energy Decisions, Markets, and Policies (Spring 2012)

An undergraduate social-science survey course (Sloan School of Management, jointly offered with Economics, Anthropology, and Urban Studies and Planning; the OCW HTML pages show only the number 15.031J, while the project's verification record lists the additional numbers 14.43J, 21A.341J and 11.161J taken from the course data.json; a core subject of MIT's Energy Studies Minor) on how energy choices are made at every level from the individual household up to firms, movements and governments, drawing on economics, management, political science, sociology and anthropology. Six units run from an overview of the U.S. and comparative energy systems, through the climate problem (including a simulation-based negotiation role play), household and business decision-making (energy demand economics, financial and normative frameworks, cases on combined heat and power and biodiesel, strategy and busi…

Stated prerequisites (paraphrased): 14.01 Principles of Microeconomics is stated on the syllabus page as the prerequisite for the course.

## 3. Gaps

### 3.1 Concepts the MIT baseline covers that AIPE has no lesson for yet

- 'Full sun hours' (peak-sun-hours) module rating and first-order daily energy-balance sizing of array and battery (S4.2, S7.1): re.system-design names sizing generically, but no module or lesson names… (MIT EC.S07)
- 2x2 linear systems: eigenvalues and eigenvectors, normal modes, fundamental matrix, trace-determinant classification of phase portraits, matrix exponential, variation of parameters. The f.linear-alge… (MIT 18.03SC)
- A Foundations-level mixed analog/digital build project (Lab 4 Audio playback system; syllabus learning outcome 16 on designing, building and testing an audio playback system; the mandatory Homework 1… (MIT 6.002)
- A hands-on I-V measurement lab built around a low-cost Arduino sourcemeter (course Lec 7 lab page). (MIT 2.627)
- A hands-on feedback laboratory sequence with a motor mechanism, prelabs and a take-home quiz (Lab 1). AIPE currently has only software-only Python labs (labs/buck_open.py ideal CCM calculation, labs/… (MIT 6.302)
- A multi-module capstone design project (power stage, filters, devices, heatsink and controller selected from datasheets; design-project page). The G3 gate in taxonomy.json requires a design with spec… (MIT 6.622)
- Acoustic waves and acoustic boundary-value problems (outside AIPE Academy's power and energy scope; recorded for completeness, no lesson recommended) (MIT 6.013)
- Activation overpotential and Butler-Volmer / Tafel kinetics, exchange current density and electrocatalysis - no AIPE coverage. (MIT 10.626)
- Amplifier frequency response methods: Miller effect and the open-circuit time-constant technique (L23-L25, R23, R24) (MIT 6.012)
- Amplitude modulation and demodulation (communications framing); only its PWM analogue is relevant to AIPE (MIT 6.003)
- Analog amplifier building blocks: common-source and other single-stage MOSFET/BJT amplifiers, multistage and cascode amplifiers, current sources/sinks and mirrors (L19-L22, R19-R21); relevant to op-a… (MIT 6.012)
- Analytic design evaluation of induction machines (machine design, Ch10) (MIT 6.061)
- Analytic design evaluation, design synthesis and optimisation of machines (MIT 6.685)
- Antennas and radiation: dipole radiation, radiation resistance, arrays and antenna gain, wireless and optical links (MIT 6.013)
- Architectural/structural considerations of grid-connected (building-mounted) PV (S12.1). (MIT EC.S07)
- Array orientation and tracking: annual output as a function of tilt/azimuth and of tracking (S5.2); not named in re.pv-systems. (MIT EC.S07)
- Audio-CD style end-to-end sampling/quantization/reconstruction capstone (AIPE has no signal-processing capstone) (MIT 6.003)

### 3.2 Modern topics AIPE needs that the MIT baseline does not cover

Required by v0.2 and confirmed absent from the listed course pages (do not attribute them to the older courses):

- Inverter-dominated grids
- Grid-forming converters
- Converter interaction and stability
- DC distribution and DC grids
- High-renewable-penetration power systems

- Real passive components: ESR, ESL, ripple-current ratings, saturation and tolerance (pe.passives) (noted for MIT 6.002)
- Recursive least squares and Kalman-type state estimation (grid state estimation, battery state-of-charge estimation) (noted for MIT 18.06SC)
- Reliability, lifetime and thermal-cycling considerations in converter design (pe.thermal, pe.practical). (noted for MIT 6.334)

- Renewable forecasting, demand response, EV charging and vehicle-to-grid, and data-driven energy management (6b lists pricing during demand peaks and valleys as a 2010 antecedent of demand response) (noted for MIT 22.081J)
- Renewable-energy and storage interfaces: PV maximum power point tracking converters, battery charging and bidirectional storage converters (re.mppt, re.storage, re.pv-systems). (noted for MIT 6.334)
- Reproducible open-source computation (Python/NumPy/SciPy/Matplotlib, ngspice) in place of the 2010 Java-applet Mathlets for visualising surfaces, contours, vector fields and flux (noted for MIT 18.02SC)
- Reproducible, open-source simulation workflows (Python/ngspice) as the learning backbone, with verification against hand analysis. (noted for MIT EC.S07)
- Resource adequacy and reliability metrics under high variable-renewable shares (LOLE, ELCC) (noted for MIT 15.031J)
- Scriptable open-source PV system modelling: pvlib-style irradiance transposition, module temperature models, performance ratio and degradation analysis from field data, plus post-2013 LCOE and cost d… (noted for MIT 2.627)

- State-space modelling above 2x2 order and numerical eigenvalue analysis of large systems, as needed for converter-dominated grid stability studies. (noted for MIT 18.03SC)
- State-space models with inputs and outputs (A, B, C, D matrices), controllability/observability, and state-space averaging of switching converters (noted for MIT 18.06SC)
- Switched and piecewise-linear systems, state-space averaging and small-signal models of converters (the course treats continuous LTI systems only). (noted for MIT 18.03SC)
- Switching losses, efficiency budgets and thermal management for converters (pe.losses, pe.thermal) (noted for MIT 6.002)
- Switching waveform metrics: period, duty cycle, average and RMS values (the waveform half of f.ac) (noted for MIT 6.002)
- Switching-converter steady-state analysis: volt-second and charge balance, conversion ratios, CCM/DCM (pe.topologies, pe.buck, pe.boost); power conversion appears only as one Fall 2000 lecture title… (noted for MIT 6.002)
- Synchronous machines in converter-dominated grids: inertia and system-strength comparisons with grid-forming converters, synchronous condensers (noted for MIT 6.685)
- System-level safety and reliability practice as it stands today: arc-fault and rapid-shutdown requirements, potential-induced degradation and soiling analytics with modern field data. The course's Le… (noted for MIT 2.627)
- The course description names sensors among its applications, but no session on sensing is listed; magnetic and current sensing for engineering measurement (Hall sensors, Rogowski coils, current trans… (noted for MIT 6.013)
- The energy-storage view used in converters: volt-second and charge balance as applied field-energy storage in L and C. (noted for MIT 8.02)
- Thermal and cooling design of machines with lumped thermal networks (instructor insights point to separate heat-transfer courses) (noted for MIT 6.685)
- Thermal management of batteries and converters from the electrical-design side (pe.thermal) (noted for MIT 2.60J)
- Thermal management of power devices (junction-to-case and heatsink thermal networks) (noted for MIT 6.012)
- Three-phase AC and grid-side quantities (ps.three-phase) and any renewable-energy or grid-integration topic (noted for MIT 6.002)
- Three-phase systems and complex (real/reactive/apparent) power as used in power-system analysis (ps.three-phase). Single-phase average power and power factor of the driven RLC circuit are probably tr… (noted for MIT 8.02)
- Three-phase transformations as change of basis: Clarke/Park (dq0) and symmetrical components (Fortescue); the course's change-of-basis and complex-matrix material is not applied to them (noted for MIT 18.06SC)
- Time-series sizing and techno-economics of PV-plus-storage systems (levelised cost of energy and storage): not evidenced by the listed titles; the term project allows a brief economic and policy disc… (noted for MIT 2.60J)
- Updated economics: LCOE, net metering and feed-in tariffs, time-of-use pricing, power purchase agreements and the post-2004 tax-credit landscape; the course's S13.2 'Government Subsidies and Incentiv… (noted for MIT EC.S07)
- Vector calculus and the integral forms of Maxwell's equations needed by f.electromagnetism and f.magnetism: not in 18.01SC; assigned in the catalogue to the 8.02 and 6.013 records (mit-8-02-2007, mit… (noted for MIT 18.01SC)
- Waveform analysis for switched circuits: average and RMS of non-sinusoidal waveforms, duty cycle, harmonics and a Fourier view. (noted for MIT 8.02)
- Wide-area measurement (PMU) based monitoring and modern protection schemes (ps.protection, ps.grid-control). (noted for MIT 6.691)
- Wide-bandgap devices (SiC MOSFETs, GaN HEMTs) and power-device technology after 2005: material properties, gate-drive requirements, reverse conduction without a body diode, fast switching and device-… (noted for MIT 6.012)
- Wide-bandgap devices (SiC MOSFETs, GaN HEMTs), their figures of merit, fast-edge gate-drive and dv/dt considerations (pe.devices, pe.gate-driving). (noted for MIT 6.334)
- Wide-bandgap devices (SiC, GaN): characteristics, gate-drive requirements and loss trade-offs are not named in any lecture title (AIPE pe.devices includes them) (noted for MIT 6.622)
- Wide-bandgap power devices (SiC, GaN), IGBTs and power-MOSFET datasheet parameters such as on-resistance temperature dependence, gate charge and output capacitance (pe.devices) (noted for MIT 6.002)

- Wireless power transfer and modern resonant/high-frequency applications beyond RF amplifiers (pe.resonant, pe.applications). (noted for MIT 6.334)
- Wireless power transfer and near-field magnetic coupling design (e.g. for EV charging) (noted for MIT 6.013)

### 3.3 Modules without any MIT coverage in the baseline

- `f.learning-with-ai` Learning with AI (foundations)
- `ps.converter-dominated` Converter-dominated power systems (power-systems)
- `ps.microgrids` Microgrids (power-systems)
- `ps.sst` Solid-state transformers (power-systems)
- `re.hybrid-systems` Microgrids and hybrid energy systems (renewable-energy)

## 4. Recommended lessons and simulations

Proposals recorded per course during the mapping review (full text in the JSON source); each is an AIPE suggestion, not an MIT recommendation.

### Foundations

Lessons:

- f.calculus: Slope as a rate of change, introduced from a measured capacitor voltage so that i = C dv/dt is the first derivative a learner meets (parallels Sessions 1-3) (MIT 18.01SC)
- f.calculus: Area as accumulation, with energy as the integral of power and Wh as the unit of the result (parallels Sessions 43-48) (MIT 18.01SC)
- f.calculus: The exponential function and the RC charging curve, solving the separable first-order equation and reading the time constant (parallels Sessions 17, 39-40; feeds f.dynamics) (MIT 18.01SC)
- f.calculus: Average and RMS of sinusoidal, rectified and PWM waveforms as integrals over one period (parallels Sessions 60-61, 68-69; feeds f.ac) (MIT 18.01SC)
- f.calculus: Linear approximation and differentials, linearising a diode or PV current-voltage curve around an operating point (parallels Sessions 23-26, 36; feeds pe.modelling) (MIT 18.01SC)
- f.calculus: Taylor series for exp, sin and cos, the small-ripple approximation, and Euler's formula as the entry to complex numbers and phasors (parallels Sessions 97-100) (MIT 18.01SC)
- f.calculus: Optimisation with derivatives, from maximum power transfer to the photovoltaic maximum power point (parallels Sessions 29-30; feeds re.mppt) (MIT 18.01SC)
- f.computation: Numerical integration of a sampled power waveform with rectangle, trapezoid and Simpson rules, and how error scales with step size (parallels Sessions 46, 63-64) (MIT 18.01SC)
- f.computation: Newton's method for a root, applied to the diode equation and a PV operating point (parallels Session 33) (MIT 18.01SC)
- f.signals bridge: improper integrals, integration by parts and partial fractions as the mathematics behind Laplace transform pairs (parallels Sessions 74-76, 91) (MIT 18.01SC)
- vectors-and-determinants-for-engineers (MIT 18.02SC)
- linear-systems-from-node-equations (MIT 18.02SC)
- partial-derivatives-and-linearisation (MIT 18.02SC)
- gradient-optimisation-and-least-squares (MIT 18.02SC)
- lagrange-multipliers-and-economic-dispatch (MIT 18.02SC)
- multiple-integrals-energy-and-average-values (MIT 18.02SC)
- flux-divergence-and-gauss-law (MIT 18.02SC)
- line-integrals-curl-and-faradays-law (MIT 18.02SC)
- maxwell-equations-bridge-to-circuits (MIT 18.02SC)
- diffusion-equation-and-thermal-resistance (MIT 18.02SC)
- first-order-odes-rc-rl-transients (f.dynamics, covers f.calculus): first-order linear ODE, integrating factor and time constant, step, exponential and sinusoidal inputs read off RC and RL circuits (MIT 18.03SC)
- complex-exponentials-and-sinusoidal-steady-state (f.ac): complex arithmetic, Euler's formula, complex replacement, amplitude-phase form, gain and phase lag of an RC filter (MIT 18.03SC)
- second-order-rlc-dynamics-and-resonance (f.dynamics, background for pe.resonant and pe.buck): characteristic equation, damping ratio, under/critical/over-damped responses, frequency response and practical resonance of a… (MIT 18.03SC)
- numerical-ode-methods-euler-to-rk45 (f.computation): direction fields, Euler's method, step size and error, solve_ivp, stiffness; applied to RC, RLC and an averaged buck model (MIT 18.03SC)
- laplace-transforms-and-transfer-functions (f.signals): Laplace basics, partial fractions, solving IVPs, transfer function from an ODE, poles and stability, step response (MIT 18.03SC)
- impulse-response-and-convolution (f.signals): step and delta functions, unit impulse response, convolution integral, response to a load-step train (MIT 18.03SC)
- fourier-series-and-harmonics (f.signals, background for pe.rectifiers and pe.inverters): Fourier series of square and PWM waveforms, LTI response to periodic input by superposition, THD (MIT 18.03SC)
- state-space-and-eigenvalues (f.linear-algebra): 2x2 systems x' = Ax from LC/RLC state variables, eigenvalues and eigenvectors, phase portraits, matrix exponential (MIT 18.03SC)
- linearisation-and-small-signal-models (pe.modelling bridge, background for ps.stability): equilibria, Jacobian linearisation, stability from eigenvalues, link to averaged converter models (MIT 18.03SC)
- la-01-node-equations-as-ax-b (f.linear-algebra, f.dc-circuits): write the KCL node equations of a resistive network as Ax = b, solve by elimination and interpret singular cases such as floating nodes (MIT 18.06SC)
- la-02-incidence-matrix-of-a-network (f.linear-algebra, f.dc-circuits): node-edge incidence matrix, KVL as potential differences A v, KCL as A^T y = 0, conductance matrix A^T C A; original counterpart to the MIT 'Graphs,… (MIT 18.06SC)
- la-03-rank-nullspace-and-solvability (f.linear-algebra): rank and the four subspaces applied to circuit and measurement equations; when a system has none, one or many solutions (MIT 18.06SC)
- la-04-least-squares-fitting-of-measurements (f.linear-algebra, re.pv, f.magnetism): fit resistor V-I or PV I-V data by projection; residuals, orthogonality and model error; tagged re.pv for the PV I-V example and f.magn… (MIT 18.06SC)
- la-05-eigenvalues-and-the-natural-response (f.linear-algebra, f.dynamics, f.signals): eigenvalues of an LC/RLC filter state matrix as poles, damping and oscillation frequency (MIT 18.06SC)
- la-06-matrix-exponential-and-state-space-response (f.linear-algebra, f.signals): x(t) = exp(At) x0 for a second-order filter, then discretisation x[k+1] = exp(A Ts) x[k] for digital control (MIT 18.06SC)
- la-07-energy-as-a-quadratic-form (f.linear-algebra): stored energy 1/2 x^T P x, positive definiteness and what it says about stability (MIT 18.06SC)
- la-08-svd-and-pseudoinverse-for-measurement-data (f.linear-algebra, f.computation): SVD of a measurement matrix, rank reduction and least squares via the pseudoinverse (MIT 18.06SC)
- la-09-change-of-basis-for-three-phase-quantities (f.linear-algebra, ps.three-phase, ps.faults, ps.grid-converters): Clarke/Park and symmetrical components as change-of-basis and similarity transforms (MIT 18.06SC)
- num-01-matrix-numerics-with-numpy (f.computation, f.linear-algebra): LU versus numpy.linalg.solve, condition numbers and rounding error on nearly singular nodal matrices (MIT 18.06SC)
- f.dc-circuits: 'The lumped abstraction - when a circuit model is valid and when it breaks' (mirrors L1 and L25, extended to the parasitic-dominated world of fast switching) (MIT 6.002)
- f.dc-circuits: 'Node method, superposition and Thevenin/Norton equivalents of a source and of a converter port' (MIT 6.002)
- f.electronics: 'The MOSFET as a switch: switch and switch-resistor models, on-resistance and a first datasheet reading' (bridge to pe.devices) (MIT 6.002)
- f.electronics: 'Operating point and incremental analysis of a nonlinear element' (preparing pe.modelling linearisation) (MIT 6.002)
- f.dynamics: 'RC and RL step responses: time constants from a switched circuit' (MIT 6.002)
- f.dynamics: 'Second-order RLC transients: damping, ringing and Q' (links to switching-node ringing in pe.emi-pcb) (MIT 6.002)
- f.ac: 'Phasors and complex impedance: frequency response of an LC output filter' plus a companion lesson on period, duty cycle, average and RMS values that the MIT course lacks (MIT 6.002)
- f.signals: 'Filters and frequency response: from impedance methods to a Bode plot' (adds the Bode/Laplace view MIT does not list) (MIT 6.002)
- f.signals / pe.control: 'Op-amp negative feedback: inverting, non-inverting and integrator stages as the error amplifier of a converter' (MIT 6.002)
- pe.losses: 'Energy and power in switched capacitors: static loss, CV^2 f dynamic loss and why frequency costs efficiency' (MIT 6.002)
- f.magnetism (measurement half): 'Measuring what you modelled: oscilloscope, multimeter, signal generator and discrepancy analysis' (modelled on course objective 6 and the lab programme) (MIT 6.002)
- f.electronics: 'The MOSFET inverter as a digital gate: voltage transfer characteristic, static discipline and noise margins' (bridge to pe.gate-driving logic levels; fills the digital-abstraction gap identified above) (MIT 6.002)
- lti-systems-step-and-impulse-response (f.signals): from the RC circuit of f.dynamics to a general LTI system, impulse and step response, convolution intuition (MIT 6.003)
- laplace-system-functions-poles-and-bode-plots (f.signals): system functions, pole-zero plots, Bode asymptotes and reading a Bode plot, verified in Python (MIT 6.003)
- difference-equations-and-the-z-transform (f.signals, bridge to pe.control): DT systems, delay/accumulator block diagrams, z-plane poles and fundamental modes, why digital controllers live in the z-domain (MIT 6.003)
- discrete-approximation-and-simulation-step-size (f.computation, pe.simulation): forward/backward Euler mapping of CT poles to DT poles, step-size limits and numerical error (MIT 6.003)
- fourier-series-of-converter-waveforms (f.ac, pe.inverters, pe.rectifiers): harmonics of square, triangular and PWM waveforms, RMS from harmonic sums, output-filter attenuation (MIT 6.003)
- sampling-aliasing-and-quantisation-in-converter-measurement (f.magnetism measurement half, pe.control): sampling theorem, aliasing of switching ripple, anti-alias filtering, ADC bit depth and quantisation error; taught… (MIT 6.003)
- negative-feedback-proportional-and-pd-control (f.signals to pe.control): closing a loop around first- and second-order plants, pole movement with gain, overshoot and steady-state error, before converter compensators (MIT 6.003)
- frequency-response-of-rc-and-lc-filters (f.signals, pe.inverters, pe.resonant): sweep an output filter in simulation and relate resonance and damping to pole locations (MIT 6.003)
- f.electromagnetism: From Faraday's law to Maxwell's equations - Gauss's and Ampere's laws applied to a coaxial cable, a busbar and a solenoid, deriving capacitance and inductance per metre (MIT 6.013)
- f.electromagnetism: Quasistatics - when is a circuit model valid? Electrical size versus wavelength and switching edge rates (MIT 6.013)
- f.electromagnetism: Where does the power flow? Poynting's theorem in a coaxial cable and a transmission line (lesson-level prerequisite: f.ac for the sinusoidal case) (MIT 6.013)
- f.dynamics: Charge relaxation - where C, R and L and the RC time constant come from in field quantities (epsilon/sigma) (MIT 6.013)
- f.magnetism: Magnetic circuits and reluctance - from field laws to the inductance of a gapped core, with B-H loop and saturation (MIT 6.013)
- ps.components: Transmission lines as distributed circuits - characteristic (surge) impedance, reflections and the long-line model (lesson-level prerequisites: f.electromagnetism, f.ac) (MIT 6.013)
- ps.components / pe.emi-pcb: Travelling-wave transients - lightning and switching surges, cable reflections and gate-drive ringing (shared lesson; lesson-level prerequisite: the distributed-line lesson above) (MIT 6.013)
- pe.magnetics / pe.losses: Skin depth and the AC resistance of conductors and windings (MIT 6.013)
- ps.machines: Forces and torque from stored field energy - virtual work, the Faraday disk and the synchronous machine (MIT 6.013)
- pe.emi-pcb: Why loops radiate - dipole radiation and the physics behind radiated EMI (optional enrichment; radiation is not yet in any AIPE module) (MIT 6.013)
- f.electromagnetism: From Coulomb's law to the capacitor: electric field, potential and energy stored in a capacitor (MIT 8.02)
- f.electromagnetism: Gauss's law as a flux-counting tool, with the fields around cables and bus bars as examples (MIT 8.02)
- f.dc-circuits: Current, resistance and Ohm's law from the microscopic picture to a circuit model (MIT 8.02)
- f.electromagnetism: Biot-Savart and Ampere's law: field of a straight wire, a loop, a solenoid and a toroid (MIT 8.02)
- f.electromagnetism -> ps.machines: Force and torque on a current loop: the principle behind every motor (MIT 8.02)

### Power Electronics

- f.electronics / pn-diode-from-physics-to-model: how depletion and diffusion capacitance and the exponential I-V law arise, and when the piecewise-linear diode model AIPE uses in converters is valid (academic reference:… (MIT 6.012)
- f.electronics / mosfet-operating-regions-for-switching: threshold voltage, linear (ohmic) and saturation regions, body effect, and why a power switch is driven hard into the linear region (reference L9-L11) (MIT 6.012)

- pe.devices / minority-carrier-storage-and-reverse-recovery: diode charge storage and diffusion capacitance as the physics behind reverse-recovery loss in converters (reference L16) (MIT 6.012)
- pe.devices / from-bjt-to-igbt: BJT forward-active and saturation regimes as the bridge to IGBT conduction, saturation voltage and tail current (reference L17-L18) (MIT 6.012)
- pe.losses / switching-loss-from-the-cmos-inverter: energy-per-transition and CV^2f dynamic power as the archetype of capacitive switching loss, extended to a half-bridge (reference L14, R15) (MIT 6.012)

### Renewable Energy

Lessons:

- electrochemical-cell-fundamentals (re.batteries; curriculum/renewable-energy/energy-storage/): galvanic cell anatomy, why a cell has a voltage, Nernst equation at the level of 'voltage depends on composition', OCV versu… (MIT 10.626)
- battery-equivalent-circuit-models (re.batteries, f.dynamics): Thevenin and 2-RC cell models, internal resistance, time constants, identifying parameters from a pulse test - mirrors MIT session 4. (MIT 10.626)
- battery-impedance-spectroscopy-basics (re.batteries, f.ac, f.signals): EIS as a frequency response, Nyquist plots, reading series, charge-transfer and diffusion regions - mirrors MIT sessions 5-7 and 27. (MIT 10.626)
- overpotentials-and-rate-capability (re.batteries): activation (Butler-Volmer), ohmic and concentration overpotentials; polarisation curve; why usable capacity falls at high C-rate - mirrors MIT sessions 17-18 and 25-26. (MIT 10.626)
- supercapacitors-and-double-layers (re.storage, pe.passives): double-layer capacitance, pseudocapacitance, ESR, energy versus power - mirrors MIT sessions 13, 35, 37-38. (MIT 10.626)
- fuel-cell-fundamentals (home module is a taxonomy decision to flag: fuel cells are a conversion device and taxonomy.json has no fuel-cell or hydrogen node, so the lesson belongs under re.conversion or a new energy-stora… (MIT 10.626)
- energy-storage-landscape-and-ragone (re.storage): energy and power ratings, round-trip efficiency, scaling of energy versus power, comparison across technologies - extends MIT session 37 to the system view. (MIT 10.626)
- battery-management-fundamentals (re.batteries): SoC estimation on an equivalent-circuit model, balancing and protection - AIPE-original gap filler, not sourced from the MIT course. (MIT 10.626)
- lithium-ion-phase-separation-sidebar (re.batteries, advanced optional): why LFP-type electrodes show flat voltage plateaus - a short advanced note pointing to MIT sessions 15 and 20-21 as further reading. (MIT 10.626)
- re.conversion: Reading a national energy balance - primary, final and useful energy, units and conversion losses (MIT 15.031J)
- re.conversion: Finite resources - reserves, production profiles and why prices shape extraction (MIT 15.031J)
- re.system-design: Discounting, NPV and payback for an energy project (worked CHP / waste-heat recovery example using AIPE's own illustrative plant parameters, not the data of the MIT Sloan Waste Heat Recovery at Hexion… (MIT 15.031J)
- re.system-design: LCOE and LCOS - from capital cost, capacity factor and discount rate to cost per kWh (MIT 15.031J)
- ps.grid-control: How the electricity industry is organised - vertically integrated utilities, restructured markets and regulators (MIT 15.031J)
- ps.grid-control: Merit-order dispatch and marginal pricing - a toy electricity market with wind (MIT 15.031J)
- re.energy-management: Energy demand basics - load shapes, elasticities and demand-response economics (MIT 15.031J)
- re.integration: Policy instruments and grid codes for renewables - how incentives and standards change dispatch and interconnection (MIT 15.031J)
- Cross-module primer (prerequisite for the lessons above): Microeconomics for engineers - supply, demand, marginal cost and externalities in one lesson. No economics/markets node exists among the 57 taxonomy modules, so… (MIT 15.031J)
- re.conversion: Energy conversion chains and efficiency: from fuel or resource to electricity, with first-law efficiency of each stage and overall efficiency of the chain (MIT 2.60J)
- re.conversion: Why heat engines have a ceiling: Carnot and practical limits of thermo-mechanical conversion, and what that means for solar thermal, geothermal and biomass plants (MIT 2.60J)
- re.conversion: Minimum work to separate a gas mixture, as the physics behind CO2 capture and air separation energy penalties (MIT 2.60J)
- re.storage: Comparing storage technologies (thermal, chemical, electrochemical, mechanical) by energy density, power density and round-trip efficiency (MIT 2.60J)
- re.storage: Hydrogen as a storage pathway: electrolysis, compression or liquefaction, and fuel-cell reconversion, with the round-trip efficiency budget (MIT 2.60J)
- re.storage: Compressed and liquefied gas storage: the work of compression and liquefaction versus stored energy (MIT 2.60J)
- re.batteries: From electrochemical thermodynamics to cell voltage: open-circuit voltage, reaction energy and theoretical energy density (MIT 2.60J)
- re.batteries: Electrochemical converters at finite current: polarisation losses in fuel cells, electrolysers and batteries and the resulting efficiency (MIT 2.60J)
- re.pv: Photovoltaics as an energy-conversion process: spectrum, bandgap and conversion efficiency limits before the circuit view (MIT 2.60J)
- re.wind: Power in the wind and the Betz limit as the resource-side foundation of re.wind (MIT 2.60J)
- re.system-design: A term-project style design analysis: choose a storage technology for an application and scale, and justify it with efficiency, power range and competing options (MIT 2.60J)

- NEW re.pv-systems L1: Cells to modules to arrays - mismatch, bypass diodes, shading and module reliability (course Lec 19 / 2011 video L17). (MIT 2.627)

- re.pv: Solar resource, photovoltaic and solar-thermal conversion routes compared (MIT 22.081J)

## 6. Content completion versus academic coverage

| AIPE module status | Modules |
| --- | --- |
| available | 8 |
| draft | 3 |
| outline | 14 |
| planned | 32 |

52 of 57 modules have at least one MIT course mapped to them; 8 have an available AIPE lesson. Academic coverage does not imply content completion.
